Integrate the remaining terms of Equation <a href="#spec:resolvent-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:resolvent-series">[spec:resolvent-series]</a> and use Equation <a href="#spec:model-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:model-resolvent">[spec:model-resolvent]</a>. On the window this gives <a id="spec:layer-norm"></a>$$\|P_iE_P(t)\|
       \leq\frac{r_0C_\chi}{1-\theta}\theta^k
       \leq4\theta^{\operatorname{dist}(i,J(t))}.
 \label{spec:layer-norm}$$ This estimate is also valid when $k=0$; there is then no vanishing initial segment to remove.

<a id="spec:layer-trace"></a>
Let $\mathfrak t_t$ denote the identity trace, summed over all base and matrix indices of the fiber. It applies to the countable amplification by Lemma <a href="#nil:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nil:trace">32</a>. Since $P_i$ and $E_P(t)$ are equivariant, the adjoint-square identity and positivity imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathfrak t_t(P_iE_P(t)P_i)
   &=\mathfrak t_t(E_P(t)P_iE_P(t))\notag\\
   &\leq\|P_iE_P(t)\|^2\,\mathfrak t_t(E_P(t))\notag\\
   &\leq16\theta^{2\operatorname{dist}(i,J(t))}
                    \operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{id}}p(t).
 \label{spec:layer-trace}
\end{aligned}$$ The middle inequality follows from the operator inequality $0\leq E_P(t)P_iE_P(t)\leq\|P_iE_P(t)\|^2E_P(t)$. Thus it is the *total trace of the projection*, rather than the dimension of the layer, that occurs.

The elementary bound $|H_i-H_j|\leq|i-j|$ implies $\mathfrak w_i\leq\mathfrak w_j2^{C_\bullet|i-j|}$. Assign every integer to one of its closest points in the nonempty set $J(t)$. At most two integers at each positive distance can be assigned to a given point. Since $|J(t)|\leq2$, Equation <a href="#spec:eta-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:eta-choice">[spec:eta-choice]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_i\mathfrak w_i\theta^{2\operatorname{dist}(i,J(t))}
 &\leq2\max_{j\in J(t)}\mathfrak w_j
          \left(1+2\sum_{k\geq1}
                       (2^{C_\bullet}\theta^2)^k\right)\\
 &\leq6\max_{j\in J(t)}\mathfrak w_j.
\end{aligned}$$ Multiplying Equation <a href="#spec:layer-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:layer-trace">[spec:layer-trace]</a> by $\mathfrak w_i$ and summing proves a pointwise bound of $96\mu_\ell$ for the weighted fiber trace. The projection vanishes off the window by Equation <a href="#spec:exact-fiber-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:exact-fiber-trace">[spec:exact-fiber-trace]</a>. Central Fourier decomposition identifies $e_v$ with the integral of its fiber identity diagonal. Tonelli’s Theorem applies to the nonnegative diagonals and sums, so integrating over the probability circle proves Equation <a href="#spec:S-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:S-small">[spec:S-small]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-51"></a>
#### An actual element of the matrix reduced group algebra

<a id="spec:Cayley-operator"></a>
Use the finite alphabet supplied by Theorem <a href="#sam:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:realization">41</a> to form the group $\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$ of Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a>. For a positive label $\sigma$, let $W_\sigma$ be its matrix weight and let $D_\sigma=D_{ik}$ be its label-determined degree normalizer. Put $$w_\sigma=(dD_\sigma)^{-1/2}W_\sigma,\qquad
 (T\xi)(g)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}
       \big(w_\sigma\xi(g\sigma)+w_\sigma^*\xi(g\sigma^{-1})\big).
 \label{spec:Cayley-operator}$$ This is a finite sum of bounded matrix coefficients times right regular translations. It is self-adjoint, and inversion of the group coordinate identifies it with an element of $M_N(\mathbb C[\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}])$ in the left regular convention. In particular $T\in M_N(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))$. We use the right convention in the proof, so that $T$ commutes with left translations of $\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$.

<a id="spec:copy-diagonal"></a>
For an indexed copy $Q$, let $T_Q$ be the adjacency using just its own formal edges. The embedding and labels identify it with the restriction of $T_P$ to the source component. Let $E_Q$ be its upper spectral projection on $\mathcal C_{r_0}$. Equation <a href="#spec:cover-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:cover-gap">[spec:cover-gap]</a> holds for $T_Q,E_Q$, and if an incidence $(Q,u)$ has base type $v$, $$\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathbb C^N}E_Q(u,u)=e_v.
 \label{spec:copy-diagonal}$$ The equation follows from component reduction and deck equivariance in Proposition <a href="#spec:cover-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:cover-projection">47</a>; it does not require the connected component to be invariant under every deck translation.

<a id="stmt-spec-copy-cut"></a>
<div id="spec:copy-cut" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:ball-HS"></a>
**Lemma 49** (Finite trimming costs little operator norm). *Let $D_Q$ and $B(Q)$ be the assigned domain and trimming ball from Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a>. Then $$\|\mathbf1_{B(Q)}E_Q\|^2\leq\mathcal S.
 \label{spec:ball-HS}$$ For $\mathcal S\leq1/4$, the compression of $T_Q$ to $\ell^2(D_Q)\otimes\mathbb C^N$ is within $\alpha+20\sqrt{\mathcal S}$ of an operator $C_Q$ which is $1$ on a projection $F_Q$ equivalent to $E_Q$, has norm at most $2$, and is at most $1-\chi+\alpha$ on the complementary space. In particular $F_Q\ne0$ whenever $E_Q\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-copy-cut"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-copy-cut</summary>

*Proof.* The ball is finite. For the ordinary Hilbert–Schmidt norm on vertex and matrix indices, the projection identity gives $$\|\mathbf1_{B(Q)}E_Q\|^2
 \leq\|\mathbf1_{B(Q)}E_Q\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_{u\in B(Q)}\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathbb C^N}E_Q(u,u).$$ A fixed base vertex $v$ can occur at multiple lifts in the ball. Its multiplicity is at most $|B(Q)|$, and whenever it occurs in layer $i$, the ball bound in Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a> gives $|B(Q)|\leq\mathfrak w_i$. Grouping the nonnegative sum by base vertex, using Equation <a href="#spec:copy-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:copy-diagonal">[spec:copy-diagonal]</a>, bounds it by $\sum_i\mathfrak w_i\sum_{v\in A_i\cup B_i}e_v=\mathcal S$. This proves Equation <a href="#spec:ball-HS" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:ball-HS">[spec:ball-HS]</a> with repetitions included.

The missing vertices $V(Q)\setminus D_Q$ lie in $B(Q)$. Apply Lemma <a href="#spec:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:compression">45</a> with $A=T_Q$, $E=E_Q$, $J=\mathbf1_{D_Q}$, $a=\alpha$, $b=\chi-\alpha$, and $\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{\mathcal S}$. Its operator $B_J$ is the required $C_Q$ and its projection $E^J$ is $F_Q$. The explicit partial isometry in Lemma <a href="#spec:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:compression">45</a> proves the nonvanishing assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-forest-norm"></a>
<div id="spec:forest-norm" class="lemma">

**Lemma 50** (Matrix adjacency on a forest). *Suppose a countable forest has maximum degree at most $m_1$, and a self-adjoint adjacency on it has matrix edge weights of norms at most $w_1$. Its norm is at most $2w_1\sqrt{m_1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-forest-norm"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-forest-norm</summary>

*Proof.* Choose one root in each tree and orient every edge from parent to child. Let $B$ carry the contribution from the parent coordinate to the child coordinate, with the corresponding matrix weight. Every nonroot vertex has only one parent. Hence for a finitely supported vector $\xi$, $$\|B\xi\|^2
   \leq w_1^2\sum_x\#\{\text{children of }x\}\,\|\xi(x)\|^2
   \leq m_1w_1^2\|\xi\|^2.$$ The self-adjoint adjacency is $B+B^*$, which proves the assertion first on finitely supported vectors and then by bounded extension. Infinite trees cause no extra term; each connected tree is rooted at an ordinary vertex and its finite-distance parent relation is well-defined. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-global-gap"></a>
<div id="spec:global-gap" class="proposition">

<a id="spec:global-spectrum"></a>
<a id="spec:p0"></a>
**Proposition 51** (A nonzero upper band in the reduced representation). *For sufficiently large $\ell$, the operator in Equation <a href="#spec:Cayley-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:Cayley-operator">[spec:Cayley-operator]</a> satisfies $$\operatorname{spec}T
 \subset(-\infty,1-\chi+4\alpha]\,
             \cup[1-3\alpha,1+3\alpha].
 \label{spec:global-spectrum}$$ The second spectral part is nonempty. Its projection $$p_0=\mathbf1_{[1-\chi/4,\infty)}(T)
                    \in M_N(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))
 \label{spec:p0}$$ is consequently nonzero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-global-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-global-gap</summary>

<a id="spec:S-final-choice"></a>
*Proof.* Increase $\ell$ so that $$\mathcal S<\min\{1/4,1/1000\},\qquad
                    20\sqrt{\mathcal S}\leq\alpha.
 \label{spec:S-final-choice}$$ The assigned domains are disjoint. Form the bounded orthogonal direct sum $$C=\bigoplus_Q C_Q\oplus0,
       \qquad F_D=\bigoplus_Q F_Q\oplus0,$$ where the extra zero acts on all unassigned vertices. The uniform norm bound in Lemma <a href="#spec:copy-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:copy-cut">49</a> proves these are bounded operators on the ambient $\ell^2(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}})\otimes\mathbb C^N$. The direct sum $C$ is $1$ on $F_D$, has norm at most $2$, and is at most $1-\chi+\alpha$ on $1-F_D$. The zero summand satisfies this upper bound because $1-\chi+\alpha>0$.

At least one component of $P$ has a nonzero upper projection, since $E_P\ne0$. Its label maps give indexed copies, and Lemma <a href="#spec:copy-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:copy-cut">49</a> preserves the nonzero upper space in every such copy. Thus $F_D\ne0$. This argument uses the ordinary direct sum and does not assert that the domain assignment is equivariant.

<a id="spec:forest-error"></a>
Let $T_D$ denote the direct sum of the compressed $T_Q$, extended by zero on unassigned vertices. Lemma <a href="#spec:copy-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:copy-cut">49</a> gives $\|T_D-C\|\leq2\alpha$. On every formal edge internal to an assigned copy domain, $T_D$ has exactly the same matrix coefficient as $T$, because that coefficient is determined by the label. Disjointness of the domains and the formal-edge embeddings prevent double counting of these internal edges. By the forest conclusion of Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a>, the remaining formal edges form a forest. Consequently $T-T_D$ is precisely a matrix adjacency of the kind in Lemma <a href="#spec:forest-norm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:forest-norm">50</a>. Its maximum degree is at most $2|S|$ and its weight norm is at most $$w_1\leq\frac{\sqrt2 C_\eta}{\sqrt{dD}}.$$ It follows that $$f_\ell:=\|T-T_D\|
          \leq4C_\eta\sqrt{\frac{|S|}{dD}}=o(1).
 \label{spec:forest-error}$$ Take $\ell$ still larger so that $f_\ell\leq\alpha$. Then $\|T-C\|\leq3\alpha$. Lemma <a href="#spec:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:perturbation">46</a>, with $b=\chi-\alpha$ and the same $r_0=\chi/3$, proves Equation <a href="#spec:global-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:global-spectrum">[spec:global-spectrum]</a>. It also shows that the enclosed projection of $T$ has distance at most $3\alpha/(r_0-3\alpha)<1$ from the nonzero projection $F_D$, so it is nonzero. Since $\alpha=\chi/1000$, the number $1-\chi/4$ lies strictly between the two intervals in Equation <a href="#spec:global-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:global-spectrum">[spec:global-spectrum]</a>.

The represented reduced algebra is a unital $C^*$-subalgebra of the bounded operators and contains $T$. For clarity, its spectrum for this self-adjoint element equals the operator spectrum: a resolvent away from the real spectrum is obtained by continuous functional calculus, or by uniformly approximating the reciprocal on that compact spectrum with polynomials in $T$ and the identity. The function that equals zero on the lower interval and one on the upper interval is continuous on the spectrum. Continuous functional calculus therefore gives precisely the spectral projection in Equation <a href="#spec:p0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:p0">[spec:p0]</a> as an element of $M_N(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-52"></a>
#### Equivariant size of all local upper spaces

The comparison with the assigned domains proves spectral separation, but their ordering need not be equivariant. Thus the ordinary equivalence with $F_D$ does not by itself compare canonical traces. For the trace estimate we return to all indexed copies. Their upper spaces form an equivariant space of small trace, and we will show that the common kernel of the corresponding restrictions contains no nonzero vector of the global upper space.

Write $\mathcal H=\ell^2(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}})\otimes\mathbb C^N$. For a left $\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$-equivariant positive operator $Z$ on $\ell^2(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}})\otimes\mathcal K$, with a fixed countable orthonormal basis $(k_a)$ of $\mathcal K$, its identity trace is <a id="spec:equivariant-trace"></a>$$\operatorname{Tr}_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}} Z
       =\sum_a\langle Z(\delta_1\otimes k_a),
                              \delta_1\otimes k_a\rangle\in[0,\infty].
 \label{spec:equivariant-trace}$$ The size of a closed invariant subspace is the identity trace of its orthogonal projection. Lemma <a href="#nil:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nil:trace">32</a> proves basis independence, the adjoint-square trace identity, and invariance under equivariant partial isometries in these countably amplified spaces. In particular the size of $p_0\mathcal H$ is $$\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}},N}(p_0)
    =\sum_{j=1}^N\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}}((p_0)_{jj}),$$ with no division by $N$.

Consider the Hilbert space and projection <a id="spec:all-copies"></a>$$\mathcal L=\bigoplus_Q\big(\ell^2(V(Q))\otimes\mathbb C^N\big),
       \qquad\mathcal E=\bigoplus_Q E_Q.
 \label{spec:all-copies}$$ Left translation acts simultaneously on $Q$ and its vertices. The incidence bijection of Proposition <a href="#geo:incidences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:incidences">25</a> identifies this representation, including matrix indices, with <a id="spec:incidence-identification"></a>$$\mathcal L\cong\ell^2(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}})\otimes\ell^2(V(Y))\otimes\mathbb C^N.
 \label{spec:incidence-identification}$$ More explicitly, $(Q,u)$ is sent to $(u,v)$ where $v$ is its base type. For each $u\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$ and $v\in V(Y)$ there is exactly one such incidence: choose any lift of $v$, map its component with that lift sent to $u$, and use the deck-alignment equivalence in the definition of indexed copies. Different lift choices give the same incidence, and any incidence arises this way. The injective label maps ensure that the base type is unambiguous. This count is valid for disconnected covers and for copies with nontrivial, possibly infinite stabilizers. The action on the pairs $(Q,u)$ is free because it is free on their group vertex $u$; freeness of the action on the set of copies is unnecessary.

<a id="spec:all-copies-size"></a>
The operator $\mathcal E$ is equivariant in this representation. Its identity diagonal at the base type $v$ has matrix sum $e_v$, by Equation <a href="#spec:copy-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:copy-diagonal">[spec:copy-diagonal]</a>. Hence $$\operatorname{Tr}_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}}\mathcal E
     =\mathcal S_0:=\sum_{v\in V(Y)}e_v
     \leq\mathcal S<\infty.
 \label{spec:all-copies-size}$$ Thus $\mathcal U:=\mathcal E\mathcal L$ is a closed invariant space of finite identity trace, even though there are countably many copies and base vertices.

<a id="stmt-spec-lost-restrictions"></a>
<div id="spec:lost-restrictions" class="lemma">

**Lemma 52** (The total loss from earlier intersections). *Put $I_Q=V(Q)\setminus D_Q\subset B(Q)$. For every $\xi\in\mathcal H$, <a id="spec:all-copy-cut-loss"></a>$$\sum_Q\|E_Q(\xi|_{I_Q})\|^2\leq\mathcal S\|\xi\|^2,
 \label{spec:all-copy-cut-loss}$$ where restrictions are extended by zero inside their copies.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-lost-restrictions"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-lost-restrictions</summary>

*Proof.* Write $v(Q,u)=\operatorname{type}_Q(u)$ for the base type of an incidence, and $i(v)$ for the layer of a base vertex $v$. For a vector $z\in\mathbb C^N$ supported at $u\in V(Q)$, positivity of the diagonal matrix and Equation <a href="#spec:copy-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:copy-diagonal">[spec:copy-diagonal]</a> give $$\|E_Q(\delta_u\otimes z)\|^2
       =\langle E_Q(u,u)z,z\rangle
       \leq e_{v(Q,u)}\|z\|^2.$$ The set $I_Q$ is finite. Cauchy–Schwarz for its sum therefore gives <a id="spec:finite-cut-CS"></a>$$\|E_Q(\xi|_{I_Q})\|^2
       \leq |B(Q)|\sum_{u\in I_Q}
                  e_{v(Q,u)}\,\|\xi(u)\|^2.
 \label{spec:finite-cut-CS}$$ The right-hand side is zero for an empty cut. If $u\in I_Q$ has base type in layer $i$, the ball bound gives $|B(Q)|\leq\mathfrak w_i$. Rearranging nonnegative terms yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_Q\|E_Q(\xi|_{I_Q})\|^2
 &\leq\sum_{u\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}}\|\xi(u)\|^2
       \sum_{Q:u\in I_Q}|B(Q)|e_{v(Q,u)}\\
 &\leq\sum_{u\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}}\|\xi(u)\|^2
       \sum_{Q:u\in V(Q)}\mathfrak w_{i(v(Q,u))}e_{v(Q,u)}\\
 &=\mathcal S\|\xi\|^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The final equality is the all-copy incidence count at the fixed vertex $u$. In particular the factor $|B(Q)|$ from Cauchy–Schwarz is paid by the layer weight in $\mathcal S$; it is not discarded or assumed uniformly bounded. ◻

</details>

Together with disjointness of the $D_Q$, this controls the restriction map to all upper spaces.

<a id="stmt-spec-restriction"></a>
<div id="spec:restriction" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:analysis-operator"></a>
<a id="spec:kernel-restrictions"></a>
<a id="spec:kernel-complement-size"></a>
**Lemma 53** (A bounded restriction operator and a size bound). *The formula $$A\xi=\big(E_Q(\xi|_{V(Q)})\big)_Q
       \quad(\xi\in\mathcal H)
 \label{spec:analysis-operator}$$ defines a bounded equivariant operator $A:\mathcal H\to\mathcal U$, with $\|A\|\leq1+\sqrt{\mathcal S}$. Its kernel $K$ is a closed invariant subspace, and every $\xi\in K$ satisfies $$E_Q(\xi|_{V(Q)})=0\quad\text{for every indexed }Q.
 \label{spec:kernel-restrictions}$$ Moreover, $$\operatorname{Tr}_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}} P_{K^\perp}\leq\mathcal S_0.
 \label{spec:kernel-complement-size}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-restriction"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-restriction</summary>

*Proof.* Since the $D_Q$ are pairwise disjoint and each $E_Q$ is a contraction, $$\sum_Q\|E_Q(\xi|_{D_Q})\|^2
       \leq\sum_Q\|\xi|_{D_Q}\|^2\leq\|\xi\|^2.$$ Together with Lemma <a href="#spec:lost-restrictions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:lost-restrictions">52</a>, this shows that both terms on the right in $$A\xi=\big(E_Q(\xi|_{D_Q})\big)_Q
             +\big(E_Q(\xi|_{I_Q})\big)_Q$$ belong to $\mathcal L$. The triangle inequality there gives the asserted norm bound. Each coordinate belongs to the range of $E_Q$, so the image is in the closed space $\mathcal U$. Although the decomposition used to prove boundedness depends on the ordering of copies, the formula <a href="#spec:analysis-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:analysis-operator">[spec:analysis-operator]</a> does not. Left translation permutes the copies and intertwines their $E_Q$, so $A$ is equivariant. Its kernel is therefore closed and invariant, and Equation <a href="#spec:kernel-restrictions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:kernel-restrictions">[spec:kernel-restrictions]</a> follows directly from the coordinate formula.

The polar partial isometry of $A$ has initial projection $P_{K^\perp}$ and final projection onto $\overline{\operatorname{ran}A}$, a subspace of $\mathcal U$. It is equivariant by the polar-decomposition argument in Lemma <a href="#nil:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="nil:trace">32</a>. The adjoint-square identity and monotonicity of that trace, together with Equation <a href="#spec:all-copies-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:all-copies-size">[spec:all-copies-size]</a>, prove Equation <a href="#spec:kernel-complement-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:kernel-complement-size">[spec:kernel-complement-size]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-kernel-form"></a>
<div id="spec:kernel-form" class="lemma">

<a id="spec:kernel-form-bound"></a>
<a id="spec:strict-trace"></a>
**Lemma 54** (The upper global space injects into the small complement). *With the choices in Equations <a href="#spec:S-final-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:S-final-choice">[spec:S-final-choice]</a> and <a href="#spec:forest-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:forest-error">[spec:forest-error]</a>, $$\langle T\xi,\xi\rangle
       \leq(1-\chi+3\alpha)\|\xi\|^2
              \quad(\xi\in K).
 \label{spec:kernel-form-bound}$$ Consequently the bounded equivariant map $P_{K^\perp}:p_0\mathcal H\to K^\perp$ is injective, and $$0<\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}},N}(p_0)
               \leq\mathcal S_0\leq\mathcal S<1/2.
 \label{spec:strict-trace}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-kernel-form"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-kernel-form</summary>

<a id="spec:domain-upper-loss"></a>
*Proof.* For $\xi\in K$, Equation <a href="#spec:kernel-restrictions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:kernel-restrictions">[spec:kernel-restrictions]</a> gives $$E_Q(\xi|_{D_Q})=-E_Q(\xi|_{I_Q}).$$ Lemma <a href="#spec:lost-restrictions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:lost-restrictions">52</a> therefore implies $$\sum_Q\|E_Q(\xi|_{D_Q})\|^2\leq\mathcal S\|\xi\|^2.
 \label{spec:domain-upper-loss}$$ The local spectral estimates in Equation <a href="#spec:cover-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:cover-gap">[spec:cover-gap]</a> imply, for every vector $y$ on $Q$, $$\langle T_Qy,y\rangle
   \leq(1-\chi+\alpha)\|y\|^2+\chi\|E_Qy\|^2.$$ Apply this to the zero-extended restrictions to $D_Q$, sum over the disjoint domains, and use Equation <a href="#spec:domain-upper-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:domain-upper-loss">[spec:domain-upper-loss]</a>. The coefficient $1-\chi+\alpha$ is positive, so the unassigned part can be included in its norm bound. Equation <a href="#spec:forest-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:forest-error">[spec:forest-error]</a> gives $$\langle T\xi,\xi\rangle
 \leq(1-\chi+\alpha+\chi\mathcal S+f_\ell)\|\xi\|^2
 \leq(1-\chi+3\alpha)\|\xi\|^2,$$ because $\mathcal S\leq1/1000$ and $f_\ell\leq\alpha$. This proves Equation <a href="#spec:kernel-form-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:kernel-form-bound">[spec:kernel-form-bound]</a> for every $\xi\in K$ directly; no approximation by finitely supported kernel vectors is used.

On $p_0\mathcal H$, Equation <a href="#spec:global-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:global-spectrum">[spec:global-spectrum]</a> gives $\langle T\xi,\xi\rangle\geq(1-3\alpha)\|\xi\|^2$. Since $1-3\alpha>1-\chi+3\alpha$, this upper space intersects $K$ only at zero. The map $P_{K^\perp}$ restricted to that space is therefore injective. Both projections are equivariant, so its polar decomposition identifies $p_0\mathcal H$ with a closed invariant subspace of $K^\perp$. Equations <a href="#spec:kernel-complement-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:kernel-complement-size">[spec:kernel-complement-size]</a> and <a href="#spec:all-copies-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:all-copies-size">[spec:all-copies-size]</a> prove the non-strict upper bounds in Equation <a href="#spec:strict-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:strict-trace">[spec:strict-trace]</a>.

Finally a nonzero equivariant projection on $\ell^2(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}})\otimes\mathbb C^N$ has strictly positive identity trace. If all its identity diagonal entries were zero, their values as squared projection-column norms would make all columns at the identity zero; equivariance would make every group translate of those columns zero, and hence the projection itself zero. Proposition <a href="#spec:global-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:global-gap">51</a> proved $p_0\ne0$, so the lower bound is strict. The final upper bound follows from Equation <a href="#spec:S-final-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:S-final-choice">[spec:S-final-choice]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-spec-matrix-projection"></a>
<div id="spec:matrix-projection" class="theorem">

**Theorem 55** (The matrix projection supplied by the construction). *For the fixed choices made above and some sufficiently large integer $\ell$, the construction defines a finitely generated torsion-free group $\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$, a positive integer $N$, and the self-adjoint finite group-algebra matrix $T$ in Equation <a href="#spec:Cayley-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:Cayley-operator">[spec:Cayley-operator]</a>. Its spectrum in the reduced representation has the separation in Equation <a href="#spec:global-spectrum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:global-spectrum">[spec:global-spectrum]</a>. The resulting projection $p_0$ in Equation <a href="#spec:p0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:p0">[spec:p0]</a> belongs to $M_N(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))$ and obeys $$0<\sum_{j=1}^N\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}}((p_0)_{jj})<\frac12.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-matrix-projection"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-matrix-projection</summary>

*Proof.* The alphabet is finite by Theorem <a href="#sam:realization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sam:realization">41</a>, so the group presentation in Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a> has finitely many generators; Theorem <a href="#geo:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:main">15</a> proves torsion-freeness. Equation <a href="#spec:Cayley-operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:Cayley-operator">[spec:Cayley-operator]</a> is a finite sum in the actual reduced regular representation. Proposition <a href="#spec:global-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:global-gap">51</a> proves its spectral separation and the reduced-algebra membership and nonvanishing of $p_0$. Lemma <a href="#spec:kernel-form" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:kernel-form">54</a> supplies the stated unnormalized trace inequality. The order of choices is consistent: the geometry and transverse parameters are fixed first; Equation <a href="#spec:eta-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="spec:eta-choice">[spec:eta-choice]</a> then fixes a single accuracy; the uniform vanishing of $\beta_\ell$, $\mu_\ell$, and $|S|/(dD)$ permits all subsequent displayed inequalities by one sufficiently large $\ell$. No limit group or limit projection is taken in this final step. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Completion of the construction

<details id="proof-stmt-spec-matrix-projection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-spec-matrix-projection</summary>

*Proof of Theorems <a href="#setup:matrix-target" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:matrix-target">4</a> and <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Theorem <a href="#spec:matrix-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="spec:matrix-projection">55</a> establishes the matrix target of Theorem <a href="#setup:matrix-target" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="setup:matrix-target">4</a>. It supplies a finitely generated torsion-free group $\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}$, a finite matrix size $N$, and a projection $p_0\in M_N(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))$ with $$0<\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}},N}(p_0)<\frac12.$$ Now set $G_{\mathrm{proj}}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}*\mathbb F_N$ and apply Proposition <a href="#scalar:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:compression">3</a>. With $U=(\lambda_{G_{\mathrm{proj}}}(t_1),\ldots,\lambda_{G_{\mathrm{proj}}}(t_N))$, it gives the scalar projection $$e=(Up_0)
 (p_0U^*Up_0)^{-1}_{p_0M_N(C_r^*(G_{\mathrm{proj}}))p_0}
 (Up_0)^*
 \in C_r^*(G_{\mathrm{proj}}).$$ The same Proposition proves that $G_{\mathrm{proj}}$ is finitely generated and torsion-free and that $\tau_{G_{\mathrm{proj}}}(e)=\tau_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}},N}(p_0)\in(0,1/2)$. Thus $e\ne0,1$, proving Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

The nonintegral trace also places $[p_0]\in K_0(C_r^*(\Gamma_{\mathrm{proj}}))$ outside the image of the degree-zero reduced assembly map with trivial coefficients, by (Baum, Guentner, and Willett 2016, Proposition 10.3) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[BGW2016](#ref-BGW2016)].

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The constant-vector part of the model at a parameter with two active layers. The blue connections represent the four nonzero blocks $h_{j}(t)h_{k}(t)p(t)$, not individual graph edges. Their symmetric upper space is given by Equation [spec:model-isometry]. As the parameter approaches either endpoint, the active layers escape in the indicated direction and the fiber trace tends to zero at the weighted rate of Proposition 39.",
      "figure_id": "fig:moving-band",
      "pdf_page": 52,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:moving-band",
          "exact_quote": "The constant-vector part of the model at a parameter with two\nactive layers. The blue connections represent the four nonzero blocks\n\\(h_j(t)h_k(t)p(t)\\), not individual graph edges. Their symmetric upper\nspace is given by Equation~\\eqref{sp",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,scale=0.94,every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n\\fill[blue!7,rounded corners] (2.55,-0.5) rectangle (7.45,2.1);\n\\node[anchor=east] at (-0.2,1.4) {$A$-constants};\n\\node[anchor=east] at (-0.2,0) {$B$-constants};\n\\foreach \\idx/\\x/\\lab in {0/1/{i-1},1/3.7/{i},2/6.3/{i+1},3/9/{i+2}} {\n \\node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=3pt] (a\\idx) at (\\x,1.4) {$a_{\\lab}$};\n \\node[circle,draw,fill=white,inner sep=3pt] (b\\idx) at (\\x,0) {$b_{\\lab}$};\n \\node at (\\x,-0.85) {layer $\\lab$};\n}\n\\draw[blue!70!black,thick] (a1)--(b1);\n\\draw[blue!70!black,thick] (a1)--(b2);\n\\draw[blue!70!black,thick] (a2)--(b1);\n\\draw[blue!70!black,thick] (a2)--(b2);\n\\node[blue!70!black] at (5,2.45) {$J(t)=\\{i,i+1\\}$};\n\\draw[<-,gray] (0.5,-1.7)--(3.7,-1.7);\n\\draw[->,gray] (6.3,-1.7)--(9.5,-1.7);\n\\node[align=center] at (5,-1.7) {active layers};\n\\node at (1.7,-2.2) {$t\\downarrow0:\\ b(t)\\to-\\infty$};\n\\node at (8.3,-2.2) {$t\\uparrow\\delta:\\ b(t)\\to+\\infty$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Diagram displaying four columns representing layers: layer $i - 1$, layer $i$, layer $i + 1$, and layer $i + 2$. Each column has an upper circular node labelled $a$ ($a_{i-1}, a_i, a_{i+1}, a_{i+2}$, labelled on the left as \"A-constants\") and a lower circular node labelled $b$ ($b_{i-1}, b_i, b_{i+1}, b_{i+2}$, labelled on the left as \"B-constants\"). A shaded lavender/light-purple rounded rectangle with header label $J(t) = \\{i, i + 1\\}$ encloses the four nodes $a_i, a_{i+1}, b_i, b_{i+1}$. Inside this region, blue lines connect $a_i$ to $b_i$, $a_{i+1}$ to $b_{i+1}$, $a_i$ to $b_{i+1}$, and $b_i$ to $a_{i+1}$. Below the columns for layer $i$ and layer $i+1$, a horizontal segment with arrowheads on both ends is labelled \"active layers\". Below this, an arrow points left with label \"$t \\downarrow 0 : b(t) \\to -\\infty$\", and an arrow points right with label \"$t \\uparrow \\delta : b(t) \\to +\\infty$\"."
    }
  ],
```

```json
{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both geometric arguments establish uniform vertex thinness for geodesic triangles in a coned graph. The target excerpt does not establish the same numerical value.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-geo-thin",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-285.2",
      "source_quote": "Let a connected graph with unit edge lengths have the conclusion of Lemma <a href=\"#geo:chain-filling\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"geo:chain-filling\">19</a>. Every geodesic triangle on its vertices is $D_0$-thin, where one may take $D_0=10^6$. Thinness here measures distance to the vertices on the other two sides.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-item-simple-cayley",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-285.3",
      "target_quote": "There is an integer $D_0$, independent of all the above data, such that every vertex on a geodesic triangle in $\\widehat X$ is within $D_0$ of a vertex on another side.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The construction restricts the search for non-trivial reduced algebra projections from general discrete groups to the strictly narrower and more challenging class of torsion-free groups.",
      "relation": "specialises",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-285.2",
      "source_quote": "finitely generated torsion-free discrete group",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-285.3",
      "target_quote": "finitely generated discrete group",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
  ],
```
